_source line 855 · 2026-05-16T22:17:25.531Z_

This session is being continued from a previous conversation that ran out of context. The summary below covers the earlier portion of the conversation.

Summary:
1. Primary Request and Intent:
   I am an independent scientific reviewer for the `contrastive_trace_safety` project, operating read-only. My role is to audit experiment conclusions, verify data integrity, check code/resource compliance, and post findings to Director via `reviewer_post_to_director`. The project targets EMNLP 2026 ARR (deadline May 25, 2026) studying whether hidden-state jailbreak detectors generalize to reasoning models' self-jailbreak behavior. I review each worker completion notification, verify results on the server, and flag issues.

2. Key Technical Concepts:
   - **Commitment Point Detection**: Last safety label + 1 in HarmThoughts 16-label taxonomy
   - **HarmThoughts Dataset**: MIT-licensed, 56,931 sentence-level annotations, 1,018 traces from 4 models
   - **Static vs Temporal probes**: Single-step LR/MLP vs sliding-window (W=15) approaches
   - **Key metrics**: Coverage (K=5 crossing rate), Lead time (commitment_point - first_crossing), Pre-CP FPR, Precision, Safe FPR
   - **Cross-architecture evaluation**: r1-8b (Llama 3.1, 32 layers, 4096-d) vs r1-32b/QwQ-32B (Qwen2.5, 64 layers, 5120-d)
   - **Paper Claims after D007 pivot**: (1) HS precision advantage over text (16% vs 27-36% pre-CP FPR), (2) Temporal context necessary for both modalities, (3) Pre-textual detection remains unreliable
   - **BOCPD/CUSUM**: Bayesian Online Change-Point Detection on probe scores — failed to beat simple threshold
   - **Bootstrap CIs**: 1000 resamples on n=43 test traces — no significant differences between methods
   - **Server**: autodl-rtx6000, 2× RTX PRO 6000 Blackwell 96GB, CUDA available on cuda:0 and cuda:1

3. Files and Code Sections:
   - `/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_5/projects/contrastive_trace_safety/src/run_pipeline.py`
     - Main pipeline entry point (extract → train → eval)
     - Loads data, splits traces, trains static/temporal probes, evaluates
   
   - `/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_5/projects/contrastive_trace_safety/src/eval/evaluate.py`
     - `earliest_reliable_detection()`: 5 consecutive steps with probs>threshold AND correct
     - `first_crossing_point()`: 5 consecutive steps with probs>0.5 (no ground-truth check)
     - `detection_lead_time()`: commitment_point - earliest_detection

   - `/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_5/projects/contrastive_trace_safety/src/crta/temporal_probe.py`
     - `TemporalProbe`: 3-layer MLP on window of concatenated hidden states
     - `create_windows()`: zero-padded sliding window, flattens concatenation
     - WINDOW_SIZE=5 default, TEMPORAL_HIDDEN_DIM=512

   - `/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_5/projects/contrastive_trace_safety/src/baselines/linear_probe.py`
     - `MLPProbe`: Linear(4096,256) → ReLU → Dropout(0.1) → Linear(256,2)
     - `collect_step_features()`: extracts (hidden_state, label) pairs from .pt files

   - `/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_5/projects/contrastive_trace_safety/src/extract_hidden_states.py`
     - Uses `return_offsets_mapping=True` for correct sentence boundary detection (bug fixed)
     - Extracts [num_sentences, 13, 4096] tensor per trace

   - `/root/autodl-tmp/contrastive_trace_safety/src/experiments/bootstrap_ci.py` (on server)
     - RETRAINS models per bootstrap resample (not just resampling test traces)
     - Uses `class_weight="balanced"` in LogisticRegression
     - Paired bootstrap test: p_value = P(delta_A-B <= 0)

   - `/root/autodl-tmp/contrastive_trace_safety/src/experiments/fpr_analysis.py` (on server)
     - Uses DETECTION_WINDOW (K=5) via `first_crossing_point`
     - Evaluates text and HS probes on safe traces and pre-commitment jailbreak steps

   - `/root/autodl-tmp/contrastive_trace_safety/artifacts/exp_001b/layer_sweep_results.json` (QwQ-32B)
     - 64-layer sweep, best layer=1 (very early!), top5=[1,2,4,5,0]
     - n_train=57, n_test=19
     - Best crossing_rate=47.4%, lead=+16.3

   - `/root/autodl-tmp/contrastive_trace_safety/artifacts/exp_001a/layer_sweep_results.json` (R1-32B)
     - 64-layer sweep, best layer=2, top5=[2,1,0,20,15]
     - n_test=20
     - Best crossing_rate=55%, lead=+13.8

   - `/root/autodl-tmp/contrastive_trace_safety/artifacts/exp_001b/temporal_lr_results.json` (QwQ-32B)
     - W=5: crossing=52.6%, lead=+16.6
     - W=10: crossing=42.1%, lead=+21.9
     - W=15: crossing=47.4%, lead=+15.7

   - `paper_framing.md`: 3 claims restructured around precision advantage, evidence table with all methods
   - `ROADMAP.md`: Research phase, 32B experiments starting, DDL May 25

4. Errors and fixes:
   - **structfuse GPU contention**: structfuse training occupied cuda:0 (50GB, 100% util) allocated to this project. Posted critical finding → Director killed structfuse processes.
   - **32B downloads incomplete**: QwQ-32B had only 8/14 shards initially. Downloads continued in background and eventually completed (all 14/14 shards, 62GB total).
   - **exp-015 FPR worker failed**: No log file produced — worker crashed before execution (likely server restart). Flagged for retry → successfully re-executed.
   - **exp-014 BOCPD registry stuck at PREPARING**: Results existed on server but registry not updated. Flagged → Agent read results and updated registry.
   - **exp-006 bootstrap conclusion initially marked "success"**: Results showed NO significant differences. Agent later corrected to "negative".
   - **exp-013 text baseline conclusion marked "success"**: Oversimplification — text actually beats HS on lead time and static coverage. Posted warning about mixed results requiring framing adjustment.

5. Problem Solving:
   - **Text baseline threat (exp-013)**: Text lead time (+11.5) >> HS lead time (-1.1). Resolved by exp-015 FPR analysis showing text's advantage is inflated by high pre-CP FPR (27-36% vs HS 16%). Paper motivation preserved via precision framing.
   - **Statistical power (exp-006)**: Bootstrap shows no significant differences at n=43. Solution: cross-architecture experiments (exp-001a/b) will add traces. Consistent effects across 3+ models is evidence even without per-model significance.
   - **GRU instability**: Coverage varies 51-93% across different evaluations. Multi-seed experiments planned.
   - **Paper framing pivot (D007)**: From coverage advantage to precision advantage, driven by data showing HS and text have comparable coverage (~65%) but HS has decisively lower pre-CP FPR.
   - **32B model downloads**: Multiple attempts, disk space issues resolved by cleaning structfuse data (183GB freed), downloads eventually completed successfully (62GB each model).

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
Now let me compile the key findings:

**QwQ-32B (exp_001b):**
- GRU: mean_acc=71.3%, mean_crossing_rate=63.2% (std=12.9%), mean_lead=12.4 steps
  - Crossing rate varies wildly across seeds: 47.4%, 63.2%, 78.9% — huge instability
- Bootstrap FPR: HS pre-CP FPR = 16.4% vs Text pre-CP FPR = 31.2%
  - Bootstrap test: p=0.001, diff=-14.8%, CI [-24.8%, -4.7%] — **SIGNIFICANT!**
  - With 19 traces, this strongly supports the precision advantage claim.

**R1-32B (exp_001a):**
- GRU: mean_acc=59.5% (std=2.9%), mean_crossing_rate=66.7% (std=8.5%), mean_lead=11.2 steps
  - Crossing rate shows moderate instability across seeds: 55%, 70%, 75%
  - Accuracy is notably lower than QwQ-32B's 71.3%
- Bootstrap FPR comparison here is between temporal_lr and GRU, not HS versus text, so it's measuring something different.
  - temporal_lr FPR=27.3% vs GRU FPR=39.0% (p=0.539, not significant)
  - This is comparing two HS methods against each other, whereas exp_001b's bootstrap file directly compares HS probe (16.4%) against text baseline (31.2%) — that's the actual claim-supporting comparison I need to focus on.

For exp_001a, I'm noticing the bootstrap file doesn't include the HS vs text comparison for R1-32B, only the temporal LR vs GRU comparison. Let me check if there's a separate text baseline result for that experiment.

Looking at the key findings: QwQ-32B shows a significant FPR advantage (p=0.001) with HS at 16.4% versus text at 31.2%, which strongly supports the main claim. But for R1-32B, I'm missing that direct HS vs text comparison in the bootstrap results. The GRU instability is confirmed across both 32B models with different standard deviations, and both show their best performance in the earliest layers.

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
Both have the same set of result files. Let me also read the temporal_lr_results for R1-32B to get the full picture, and check the R1-32B layer sweep data I recalled from the summary.

Key issue: exp_001a's bootstrap_fpr_results.json compares temporal_lr vs GRU FPR (both are HS methods), not HS vs text. This means:
- For R1-32B, we don't have the HS vs text FPR comparison that supports Claim 1


- But QwQ-32B does have it (HS 16.4% vs Text 31.2%, p=0.001)

So there's a design mismatch between the two 32B experiments—exp_001a is testing which HS method works better while exp_001b is testing whether HS beats text altogether. I need to check the temporal_lr results for R1-32B to see what's actually there.
```

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
Now I have a comprehensive picture. Let me analyze the key findings:

**R1-32B (exp_001a) layer sweep:**
- Best layer=2 (score=7.6), top5=[2,1,0,20,15]
- Layer 2: acc=61.9%, crossing_rate=55%, lead_mean=13.8
- Layer 63 (last): acc=74.3%, crossing_rate=25%, lead=12.8
- The "score" metric is crossing_rate × lead_mean / something... Let me reverse-engineer:
  - Layer 2: score=7.6, crossing=0.55, lead=13.8 → 0.55 × 13.8 = 7.59 ≈ 7.6 ✓
  - Layer 0: score=5.55, crossing=0.65, lead=8.54 → 0.65 × 8.54 = 5.55 ✓
  - So score = crossing_rate × lead_mean

This is the critical issue! The scoring metric multiplies crossing rate by lead time, which inherently rewards early layers that detect something quickly—even though they have much lower accuracy (61-64%) compared to deeper layers. The early layers are being ranked highly because they trigger detections with high lead times, not because they're actually more accurate.

Looking at the concrete examples: Layer 2 scores 7.6 with only 62% accuracy, while Layer 63 has 74% accuracy but scores just 3.2 because it fires less frequently. The metric is fundamentally misaligned with what we actually want—reliable detection rather than noisy early signals that are mostly false positives.

The root issue is that the scoring system rewards early firing and high crossing rates without any penalty for being wrong. Early layers in the 32B models produce noisier representations, so probes trained on them fire earlier (often on random fluctuations before the actual commitment point) and more frequently, gaming the metric despite poor accuracy. In the smaller 8B model, the best probe came from a middle layer where representations were more meaningful, but the scoring metric is selecting for depth in the 32B models based on noise rather than signal.

Looking at the temporal LR results across different window sizes, I see that larger windows consistently improve accuracy while reducing both crossing rate and lead time — this aligns with my hypothesis that more context leads to more conservative, reliable predictions rather than noisy early triggers. The GRU baseline is barely above random for binary classification with concerning instability across seeds, and its false positive rate of 39% is substantially worse than temporal LR's 27.3%, confirming that the simpler approach is more reliable here.

Now examining the bootstrap FPR comparisons more carefully — the R1-32B results only compare methods within HS, not HS versus text, which is a critical gap. But the QwQ-32B results do show what I'm looking for: HS pre-CP achieves 16.4% FPR versus text's 31.2% with p=0.001, directly supporting the claim that HS is more effective.

I'm also noticing a fundamental problem with how I've been selecting layers — the crossing rate × lead mean score rewards noisy early detectors over accurate ones, which is backwards. Layer 2 scores highest by this metric with 62% accuracy, but layer 63 achieves 74% accuracy, so the metric is actively pushing me toward worse models. I need to reconsider what the right optimization criterion should be here.

The R1-32B GRU is another concern — 59.5% accuracy on a binary task is barely above chance, and the high variance suggests it's not actually learning meaningful patterns from those early layers. Combined with the missing HS versus text comparison for R1-32B, the precision advantage claim is only validated on QwQ-32B so far, not across both architectures as I'd need.

There's also a layer selection inconsistency that bothers me: R1-8B uses layer 14 out of 32 (middle depth), while the 32B models are pulling from layers 0-5 out of 64 (very shallow). This looks like a metric artifact rather than a real signal, and if the paper claims consistency across architectures, this discrepancy needs explanation. Let me check the registry to see if there are any updated experiments.
```

_source line 893 · 2026-05-16T22:19:23.488Z_

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
Now I have the full picture from the registry. Let me compile all my findings:

**Key findings:**

1. **CRITICAL — Score metric is `crossing_rate × lead_mean`, fundamentally flawed for layer selection:**

For R1-32B:
- Layer 2 (selected as "best"): acc=61.9%, crossing=55%, lead=13.8, score=7.6
- Layer 10: acc=70.6%, crossing=60%, lead=8.7, score=5.2
- Layer 63 (deepest): acc=74.3%, crossing=25%, lead=12.8, score=3.2


- Layer 63 (deepest): acc=74.3%, crossing=25%, lead=12.8, score=3.2

The metric is rewarding early layers that fire frequently but inaccurately—they trigger on noise and random fluctuations before the actual commitment point, earning high scores for false positives. Meanwhile, deeper layers achieve much higher accuracy but get penalized because they're more conservative and have lower crossing rates. This inverted incentive structure explains why shallow layers are being selected as "best" despite performing worse, and it also accounts for the discrepancy with R1-8B where a mid-depth layer performed better—the different dataset likely had different noise characteristics that shifted where the metric's perverse incentives peaked.

I'm also noticing some critical gaps in the experimental validation. The exp_001a results only compare two hidden state methods against each other (temporal_lr at 27.3% FPR versus GRU at 39.0% FPR), but there's no comparison between hidden state and text-based approaches for the 32B model. To properly support the claim about precision advantages across architectures, I need that HS versus text comparison on both 32B models—right now only QwQ-32B has it showing the advantage. Additionally, the registry metrics for exp_001a appear to have inconsistencies between what's recorded and what the actual layer data shows. Looking at the FPR advantage for QwQ-32B (16.4% HS vs 31.2% text, p=0.001), this does support the first claim for at least one model. But the Agent's conclusions are problematic: exp_001a (R1-32B) is marked success despite near-random accuracy and missing comparisons, plus there's suspicious metric copying in the registry. More critically, both experiments claim the hypothesis about best layers near 44% depth (~layer 28) is confirmed, but the actual data clearly shows best performance in layers 0-5 out of 64 — the hypothesis is falsified, yet marked as success.

Actually, I'm noticing something in the registry data that's concerning: exp_001a lists best_layer_crossing as 0.474 and best_layer_lead as 16.3, but those exact numbers match QwQ-32B's results, not R1-32B's actual layer 2 performance (crossing=0.55, lead=13.8). The metrics appear to have been copied between experiments.
```

{
  "text": "[severity: critical] 审查报告 — exp_001a (R1-32B) + exp_001b (QwQ-32B)\n\n## 核心发现\n\n### 1. [critical] Layer selection metric 根本性缺陷\n\n两个 32B 实验的 `score = crossing_rate × lead_mean`，这个指标奖励\"早期频繁触发\"而非\"准确检测\"。\n\n**证据（R1-32B）：**\n- Layer 2（\"最佳\"）：acc=61.9%, crossing=55%, lead=13.8, score=7.6\n- Layer 10：acc=70.6%, crossing=60%, lead=8.7, score=5.2\n- Layer 63：acc=74.3%, crossing=25%, lead=12.8, score=3.2\n\n早层（0-5）准确率仅 61-64%（二分类接近随机），但因噪声高在 commitment point 前就触发，获得高 lead time 被指标奖励。深层准确率更高（74%）但更保守所以 crossing rate 低、score 低。\n\n**影响**：当前\"best layer = 1-2（1.6-3.1% depth）\"的结论很可能是指标伪影，不是真实信号。r1-8b 的 best layer 14/32（44% depth）可能才是合理的，而 32B 的等效 layer 应该在 layer 20-30 范围。整个 layer sweep 需要用一个包含准确率的指标重跑选择。\n\n### 2. [critical] Registry exp_001a 数据 copy-paste 错误\n\nRegistry 记录 exp_001a (R1-32B): `best_layer_crossing: 0.474, best_layer_lead: 16.3`\n实际 layer_sweep_results.json layer 2: crossing=0.55, lead=13.8\n\n`0.474` 和 `16.3` 恰好是 exp_001b (QwQ-32B) layer 1 的数据。Agent 更新 registry 时复制了错误的数字。\n\n### 3. [critical] 假设明确被证伪但结论标 \"success\"\n\n两个实验假设：\"best layer near 44% depth (~layer 28)\"\n实际结果：best layer = 1-2/64（1.6-3.1% depth）\n假设被证伪，但 conclusion=\"success\"。这误导后续决策。正确结论应为\"negative\"或\"partial\"——实验成功运行但核心假设不成立。\n\n### 4. [warning] R1-32B 缺少 HS vs text FPR 对比\n\nexp_001b (QwQ-32B) 的 bootstrap_fpr 比较 HS vs text（16.4% vs 31.2%, p=0.001）——直接支持 Claim 1。\nexp_001a (R1-32B) 的 bootstrap_fpr 比较 temporal_lr vs GRU（27.3% vs 39.0%, p=0.539）——两种 HS 方法互比，不是 HS vs text。\n\n**Claim 1（precision advantage）要 claim \"cross-architecture\"，R1-32B 上必须补 HS vs text FPR 对比。**\n\n### 5. [warning] R1-32B GRU 准确率接近随机\n\nmean acc = 59.5%（二分类 chance=50%），std=2.9%。crossing rate std=8.5%（range 55-75%）。在 score 指标选出的浅层上，GRU 几乎没学到有意义的模式。这进一步佐证 layer selection 有问题。\n\n### 6. [info] QwQ-32B FPR 优势显著\n\nHS pre-CP FPR=16.4% vs Text pre-CP FPR=31.2%, bootstrap p=0.001, 95% CI [-24.8%, -4.7%]。单独看这是 Claim 1 的强证据，但需要 R1-32B 补数据才能 claim 跨架构。\n\n## 建议动作\n\n1. **修正 layer selection 指标**：改用 `acc × crossing_rate` 或引入 FPR 惩罚，重新确定 best layer。或直接用 exp_015 已验证的 r1-8b 最佳层深度（44%）按比例映射到 32B 模型（layer ~28）做对照。\n2. **补 R1-32B HS vs text FPR 对比**：用与 exp_001b 相同的方法，在 R1-32B 上运行 bootstrap FPR。\n3. **修正 registry**：exp_001a metrics 需纠正，conclusion 需改为 \"partial\" 或 \"negative\"。\n4. **重新评估 \"cross-architecture consistency\" 论述**：当前数据不支持简单的跨架构一致性——3 个模型的 optimal depth 差异巨大（44% vs 1.6% vs 3.1%），coverage 和 accuracy 也差异大。\n",

For simple settings like theme/model, suggest the /config command.\n- keybindings-help: Use when the user wants to customize keyboard shortcuts, rebind keys, add chord bindings, or modify ~/.claude/keybindings.json. Examples: \"rebind ctrl+s\", \"add a chord shortcut\", \"change the submit key\", \"customize keybindings\".\n- simplify: Review changed code for reuse, quality, and efficiency, then fix any issues found.\n- fewer-permission-prompts: Scan your transcripts for common read-only Bash and MCP tool calls, then add a prioritized allowlist to project .claude/settings.json to reduce permission prompts.\n- loop: Run a prompt or slash command on a recurring interval (e.g. /loop 5m /foo). Omit the interval to let the model self-pace. - When the user wants to set up a recurring task, poll for status, or run something repeatedly on an interval (e.g. \"check the deploy every 5 minutes\", \"keep running /babysit-prs\"). {"result":"{\"success\": true, \"data\": {\"path\": \"/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_5/projects/contrastive_trace_safety/paper_framing.md\", \"content\": \"# Paper Framing — contrastive_trace_safety\\n\\n## Target Venue\\n- Primary: EMNLP 2026 Main (LLM Security Track)\\n- Deadline: ARR submission May 25, 2026 (9 days)\\n\\n## Core Claims (4 条, D008 升级)\\n\\n1. **Hidden-state probes achieve substantially more precise safety commitment detection than text-based monitoring** — across 3 models (r1-8b, R1-32B, QwQ-32B), hidden-state temporal probes achieve pre-commitment FPR 16% vs text 27-36% (1.7-2.2x lower). QwQ-32B paired bootstrap: p=0.001. Coverage is comparable between modalities, but hidden-state monitoring fires more accurately in the critical pre-commitment window. Unsupervised geometric methods (Mahalanobis/TrajGuard) fail entirely.\\n\\n2. **Temporal context is necessary for commitment detection regardless of signal modality** — both hidden-state and text-based probes benefit from temporal windowing (W=15). GRU sequence models push coverage further but at higher FPR cost. The temporal improvement is method-agnostic; the precision advantage is modality-specific. Validated across 3 models, 2 architectures, 2 scales.\\n\\n3. **Pre-textual detection improves with model scale but remains unreliable** — GRU lead time scales 2.5x from 8B (+4.7 steps) to 32B (+11-12 steps, stable across 3 seeds: ±0.76). However, crossing rate remains variable (63-67% on 32B) and BOCPD/CUSUM fail to improve over simple thresholds. The oracle gap persists: larger models encode commitment signals earlier, but extracting reliable pre-textual detection remains an open challenge.\\n\\n4. **Safety-relevant signal location is scale-dependent** — best probe layer shifts from mid-depth (layer 14/32 = 44% on 8B) to early layers (layer 1-2/64 = 2-3% on 32B). This is a novel analysis contribution: the safety signal is NOT at a fixed relative depth, suggesting fundamentally different internal representations across scales. Practical implication: layer selection must be model-specific.\\n\\n## Story Spine\\n\\n- **Problem**: Reasoning models exhibit \\\"self-jailbreak\\\" — safety reasoning and harmful execution interleave throughout extended CoT, culminating in a \\\"commitment point\\\" where safety reasoning permanently ceases. Existing hidden-state detectors (TrajGuard, SafeSwitch, SIREN) target adversarial INPUT attacks on instruction-tuned models; they have never been evaluated on reasoning-model self-jailbreak where the threat is the model's OWN reasoning process.\\n\\n- **Existing approaches & gaps**: Hidden-state detectors achieve 95% defense on standard LLMs (TrajGuard) but operate at token-level. Reasoning-model safety analysis (Self-Jailbreaking, HarmThoughts) characterizes the phenomenon without providing deployable detectors. Text-level CoT monitors (SafeRemind, SafeThink) are vulnerable to pathological CoT and, as we show, produce higher false positive rates in the pre-commitment window.\\n\\n- **Our framing**: We provide the first systematic evaluation of detection methods for reasoning-model safety commitment across 3 models, 2 architectures, and 2 scales, discovering that: (a) hidden-state probes achieve decisively lower pre-commitment FPR than text monitoring (16% vs 27-36%, p=0.001); (b) temporal context is critical for both modalities, with GRU lead time scaling 2.5x from 8B to 32B; (c) the optimal probe layer shifts from mid-depth (44%) on 8B to early layers (2-3%) on 32B, revealing scale-dependent safety signal geometry; (d) despite improved lead time at scale, reliable pre-textual detection remains an open challenge.\\n\\n- **Headline result**: \\\"Across 3 reasoning models (8B-32B, Llama/Qwen architectures), hidden-state temporal probes detect safety commitment with 16% pre-commitment FPR vs text-based 27-36% (p=0.001). Lead time scales 2.5x with model size (+4.7 to +11-12 steps), while optimal probe depth shifts from 44% to 2-3%, revealing scale-dependent safety signal geometry.\\\"\\n\\n## Key Evidence Summary\\n\\n### r1-8b (n=43 test, class_weight=balanced pending reconciliation)\\n\\n| Method | Coverage | Lead Time | Pre-CP FPR | Precision | Safe FPR |\\n|--------|----------|-----------|------------|-----------|----------|\\n| Text static | 60.5% | +4.3 | 35.6% | 44.8% | 50.2% |\\n| Text temporal W=15 | 65.1% | +11.5* | 27.2% | 48.7% | 38.5% |\\n| HS static LR L14 | 60.5% | +3.0 | 16.2% | 62.2% | 39.0% |\\n| HS temporal LR W=15 | 65.1% | +4.9 | 16.0% | 58.3% | 39.2% |\\n| GRU multi-7L h=256 | 76-93%** | +4.7-8.4** | 28.0% | 57.3% | 61.6% |\\n\\n*Text lead time inflated by high pre-CP FPR\\n**GRU unstable across seeds; r1-8b numbers from exp-015 (possibly default class weights, to be reconciled with balanced standard)\\n\\n### 32B Cross-Architecture (D008, class_weight=balanced)\\n\\n| Model | Best Layer | GRU Lead (3-seed) | GRU Crossing | GRU Acc | HS pre-CP FPR | Text pre-CP FPR | p-value |\\n|-------|-----------|-------------------|-------------|---------|--------------|----------------|---------|\\n| R1-32B (64L) | 2 (3%) | +11.2 ±0.76 | 66.7% ±8.5% | 0.595 ±0.029 | — | — | pending |\\n| QwQ-32B (64L) | 1 (2%) | +12.4 ±0.75 | 63.2% ±12.9% | 0.713 ±0.005 | 16.4% | 31.2% | **0.001** |\\n\\n### Negative Results\\n- BOCPD lambda=10: coverage 93% but lead +3.5 < simple threshold +4.9\\n- CUSUM: all configurations worse than simple threshold\\n- TrajGuard (Mahalanobis): failed entirely\\n\\n## Venue Fit Risks\\n\\n- **EMNLP LLM Security track fit**: Strong — defense/detection evaluation for reasoning model jailbreak.\\n- **Method simplicity risk**: Mitigate: frame as empirical study with analytical contributions (scale effect, layer dynamics). The finding that lead time scales 2.5x and optimal layer shifts drastically is itself a contribution.\\n- **Statistical power**: QwQ p=0.001 resolves this for Claim 1. R1-32B HS vs text bootstrap pending. Cross-model consistency across 3 models is strong evidence.\\n- **High safe FPR risk**: All methods 39-62% safe FPR. Frame as diagnostic tool limitation; production deployment needs threshold tuning.\\n\\n## Anticipated Reviewer Concerns\\n\\n1. \\\"Method novelty?\\\" → Empirical study + analytical contributions (scale effect, layer dynamics, cross-architecture evaluation). BOCPD adds systematic method comparison.\\n2. \\\"Only one model family?\\\" → RESOLVED (D008): 3 models, 2 architectures, 2 scales.\\n3. \\\"GRU unstable?\\\" → 3-seed reporting shows lead time is stable (±0.76), crossing rate variable. Itself an informative finding.\\n4. \\\"Too few traces?\\\" → Cross-model validation + QwQ bootstrap p=0.001.\\n5. \\\"Fine-grained taxonomy?\\\" → Ablation planned (P1).\\n6. \\\"Text baseline?\\\" → RESOLVED: HS decisive precision advantage (p=0.001).\\n7. \\\"Commitment point circular?\\\" → Cross-model consistency (3 models) argues against annotation artifact.\\n8. \\\"Missing related work?\\\" → RESOLVED.\\n9. \\\"No FPR analysis?\\\" → RESOLVED.\\n10. \\\"High safe FPR?\\\" → Limitation acknowledged. HS-vs-text advantage holds.\\n11. \\\"Layer selection is model-specific — not generalizable?\\\" → NEW. Frame: the METHOD generalizes (temporal probing + layer sweep), the optimal LAYER is model-specific. This is itself a finding about how safety representations scale.\\n\\n## Title Candidates\\n- \\\"Temporal Probing for Safety Commitment Detection in Reasoning Models\\\"\\n- \\\"When Does Temporal Context Matter? Hidden-State Detection of Safety Commitment in Reasoning Models\\\"\\n- \\\"Scale-Dependent Safety Signals: Hidden-State Detection of Commitment in Reasoning Models\\\"\\n\", \"total_chars\": 7257, \"truncated\": false}, \"error\": null, \"metadata\": {}}"}

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
Now I have both the updated ROADMAP and the paper_framing.md. Let me analyze whether the framing is supported by the evidence:

**Claim 4 is the most problematic:**

> "Safety-relevant signal location is scale-dependent — best probe layer shifts from mid-depth (layer 14/32 = 44% on 8B) to early layers (layer 1-2/64 = 2-3% on 32B). This is a novel analysis contribution"

This claim is built entirely on the flawed `score = crossing_rate × lead_mean` metric! The "finding" that best layer shifts from 44% to 2-3% is an artifact of:


- A scoring metric that rewards noisy early layers
- The 32B probes on early layers having ~62% accuracy (barely above random)

The Agent has now elevated this metric artifact into a **core paper claim** ("novel analysis contribution"). This is exactly the rationalization pattern I warned about — packaging a measurement bug as a "discovery."

**Claim 3 also has issues:**

> "GRU lead time scales 2.5x from 8B (+4.7 steps) to 32B (+11-12 steps)"

But the GRU on 32B was trained on layers selected by the flawed metric (layers 0-5), so if the layer selection is wrong, the lead time scaling is built on a faulty foundation.

The GRU results are compromised because the layer selection itself is flawed. The apparent lead time improvement might just be noisier early-layer features firing earlier — more false positives masquerading as better detection. R1-32B GRU accuracy sits at 59.5%, barely above chance, so those "+11.2 step" detections are largely random noise. When you're firing at ~60% accuracy across the board, you naturally fire early because you're triggering everywhere, including well before the actual commitment point.

**ROADMAP assessment:**

The ROADMAP correctly flags "R1-32B HS vs text paired bootstrap" as a near-term priority, but it glosses over the layer selection metric problem entirely. The "新发现" section treats "Layer dynamics: mid-depth (44%) → early (2-3%)" as a genuine finding rather than acknowledging it might be an artifact of the flawed metric.

**Key concerns with the 4-claim framing:**

Claim 1 on precision advantage is the strongest — QwQ-32B's p=0.001 is solid, though R1-32B data is still missing and needs that bootstrap. Claim 2 about temporal context being necessary holds up reasonably well across conditions. Claim 3 on scale-driven lead time improvement is deeply problematic because the 2.5x gain is partly an artifact of flawed layer selection producing noisy early-layer detections, combined with R1-32B's near-random 59.5% accuracy and the fact that lead time gets inflated when calculated only on traces where detection occurs. Claim 4 on scale-dependent layer dynamics is the most vulnerable.

The core issue is that Claim 4 rests entirely on a flawed scoring metric. What's actually happening is that on 32B models, the noisiest early layers (0-5, with the lowest accuracy) score highest under the crossing_rate × lead_mean metric simply because they fire early and frequently. I flagged this metric problem in my earlier post, but the ROADMAP and framing were written around the same time or after. I need to post a follow-up showing how the framing has rationalized the metric flaw into a supposed discovery — the Agent observed that the metric picks early layers as an artifact, then repackaged that as a novel finding about layer dynamics rather than questioning the metric itself.

Now I should post a targeted warning about how Claim 4's flawed foundation undermines Claim 3 downstream.

  (c) 额外: position-only baseline: 只用 step_index / trace_length 两个特征训练 LR，测试其 coverage/lead time
  - 资源: CPU only，用现有 hidden states，~1-2h 全部模型
  - 风险评估: 如果 CP 集中在 ~83% trace 位置，position baseline 可能达 75-80%。关键是 selectivity > 0 且 precision 差异不能被 position 解释。我认为 HS vs text precision 差异 (16% vs 27-36% FPR) 不可能被 position 解释（两者使用相同 position），这是我们的安全垫。

**C3: k=3 pooled meta-analysis 不成立**
- 判定: ACCEPT
- 方案: 完全移除 meta-analysis framing。具体:
  - 删除 Abstract 中 I²=0.08-0.21 和 p=0.003
  - 主表改为 per-model results table
  - 用"cross-model consistency"叙述替代 meta-analytic conclusion
  - 保留 per-model paired bootstrap（这些是有效的）
- 工作量: Paper-only

**C4: 数据划分矛盾 (166 vs 125 train)**
- 判定: ACCEPT
- 方案: 确认实际划分。推测是 125 train / 41 val / 43 test（val 用于 layer selection + threshold tuning）。需 Agent 确认代码中的实际 split。Paper 统一报告三折 + 明确 val 用途。
- 工作量: Agent 确认 + Paper 修改

**C5: OpenThinker-7B 选择性排除**
- 判定: ACCEPT + 补实验
- 方案: OpenThinker-7B (Qwen2.5-7B base) 是 exp-011 (P2)。两个选择:
  (a) 跑 OpenThinker-7B 全 pipeline 并纳入 pooled analysis — 7B 模型 extraction 快（~30min 1 GPU），完善 2x2 矩阵
  (b) 论文中预声明排除标准（如"仅包含 distilled reasoning models"）
  - 推荐 (a)：时间允许（9 天 DDL），且补上 Qwen2.5-7B 可完善 scale 维度（7B vs 32B within Qwen family）
  - 资源: 1 GPU, ~2h

**C6: Related Work 严重不足**
- 判定: ACCEPT
- 方案: 扩展至 ~30 citations, 2 页。新增子段落:
  - Probing Classifiers: Belinkov 2017, Conneau 2018, Hewitt & Manning 2019, Hewitt & Liang 2019
  - Causal Interpretability: Meng 2022, Conmy 2023
  - Process Supervision / PRMs: Uesato 2022, Wang 2023, Lightman 2023
  - Representation Analysis: Kornblith 2019 (CKA), Bansal 2021
  - Jailbreak Detection: Robey 2023, Alon & Kamfonas 2023
- 工作量: Paper Agent 任务，需 WebSearch 验证引用真实性

---

## Should-Fix Issues Action Matrix

**S1: Hidden state 提取方式未指定**
- 方案: Paper Section 3 添加: "extract hidden state at last token of each reasoning step (sentence boundary)"。Agent 确认实际实现。

**S2: Text baseline 过弱 (TF-IDF)**
- 方案: 我们已有 sentence-BERT (all-MiniLM-L6-v2) baseline (exp-013)。检查论文是否已用 sentence embeddings 还是 TF-IDF。如果是 TF-IDF，替换为 sentence embeddings 结果。如果已是 sentence embeddings，在 paper 中明确说明。

**S3: CP 定义鲁棒性**
- 方案: Paper 增加讨论: CP oscillation cases 处理、HarmThoughts IAA 数据、CP 边界 case 分析。

**S4: Bootstrap 实施不透明**
- 方案: Paper 明确: trace-level resampling (unit=trace, not step), BCa confidence intervals, 1000 resamples, paired difference test statistic.

**S5: BOCPD 有描述无结果**
- 方案: exp-014 BOCPD 结果已有。将 BOCPD lambda=10/20/50 + CUSUM 结果表加入 Paper Section 5。负结果支撑 Claim 3。

**S6: GRU worst-seed 下界**
- 方案: 从 3-seed 数据中提取 worst-seed performance 作为 lower bound 报告。32B: lead time worst seed 约 +10.4/+11.7, crossing rate worst seed 约 58%/50%。

**S7: Transfer projection head 训练数据**
- 方案: Agent 确认 projection head 是否使用 target model labels。如果用了 → 论文明确标注为 "supervised transfer"（非 zero-shot）。

**S8: "HS > text" 非显然性论证**
- 方案: Paper 增加段落: "虽然信息论上 hidden states 理应包含更多信号，但关键贡献是量化了这个优势的 deployment-relevant magnitude: pre-CP FPR 从 27-36% 降到 16% 意味着在每 100 步监控中减少 11-20 次误报，直接影响部署可行性。"

**S9: Probing = correlation not causation**
- 方案: Limitations 增加 1 段，声明 probing 是 correlation，activation patching 可进一步验证 causality，但对 detection task correlation 已足够（R1-R2 共识）。

**S10: PRM 结构性联系**
- 方案: Related Work 新增 Process Supervision 子段落（已在 C6 覆盖）。讨论 PRM step-level supervision 与我们的 step-level probing 的联系和区别。

**S11: Cross-model transfer 分析浅**
- 方案: 两个选项:
  (a) 补跑 CKA 分析——CPU only，用现有 hidden states，~30min。但 CKA 在不同 dim (4096 vs 5120) 间需要投影，结果可能不 informative
  (b) Scope down claim: 移除 "shared geometry" 措辞，改为 "cross-model transfer achieves above-chance accuracy, suggesting partial overlap in safety-relevant representations"
  - 推荐 (b)，除非 Reviewer 认为 CKA 是 must-have

**S12: Oracle decomposition 不清晰**
- 方案: Agent 确认 62%/28%/10% 的计算方法，Paper 添加方法学描述。

---

## Format Issues

**F1**: 换用 ACL/EMNLP 官方 LaTeX 模板 — Paper Agent 最高优先级
**F2**: 添加 Responsible NLP Checklist — Paper Agent
**F3**: 修复 References 格式（完整 author names, arXiv ID）— Paper Agent
**F4**: 统一 Table 格式（GRU: mean±std not range）— Paper Agent

---

## 实验优先级

1. **C2 Control Task** (P0, 最高优先) — 如果 selectivity ≈ 0，论文核心 claim 崩塌。必须先跑。CPU ~1-2h
2. **C5 OpenThinker-7B** (P0) — 补完 2x2 矩阵。1 GPU ~2h
3. **S11 CKA** (P2) — 可选，或 scope down claim

## 关键问题请 Reviewer 回答

1. C2 control task: 你认为 position-only baseline 的预期 accuracy 是多少？selectivity 达到多少才算安全？
2. C5 OpenThinker-7B: 值不值得跑？还是预声明排除更合理？
3. S11 CKA: 是否 must-have，还是 scope down claim 更务实？
4. 整体: 修完 C1-C6 + S1-S12 后，你判断能到几分？Findings 还是有 Main track 机会？
5. 是否有我遗漏的问题？

The Director is asking me to independently review their Action Matrix in response to a Round 1 review (2.5/5 Borderline). They want me to assess: feasibility, technical details, missing items, priority ordering. They also ask 5 specific questions.

Let me analyze this systematically.

First, let me understand the context:
- Paper got 2.5/5 (borderline)
- Director created an action matrix addressing Critical (C1-C6) and Should-Fix (S1-S12) issues
- They want my independent assessment

Let me read the review concerns and the proposed actions carefully, cross-referencing with what I know about the actual experimental data.


Now looking at C2 on the control task—this is the crux of the position confounding concern. The Director's proposed solution using random-label controls and position-only baselines directly addresses whether the model is just learning positional cues rather than actual commitment detection. The safety argument holds for Claim 1 since both HS and text use the same positions, but Claims 3 and 4 about lead time and layer dynamics could still be vulnerable to this confound.

For the random-label control, that's the right methodology, and the position-only baseline is a clever check. The real question is whether position alone achieves high accuracy—if it does, that's a problem for the "safety commitment signal" interpretation. I'd need to see the actual distribution of commitment points across HarmThoughts traces to estimate what position-only accuracy would be, but the selectivity threshold should probably exceed 10 percentage points to make a convincing case that the probe is detecting something beyond position.

On C3, the k=3 meta-analysis is indefensible, and proper model reporting is the right fix. For C4, I need to double-check whether there's a separate validation fold beyond the train/test split. On C5, adding OpenThinker-7B would diversify away from Qwen, but actually using a 7B Qwen model would make the problem worse—four out of five models would then be Qwen-based. The real constraint is that HarmThoughts only includes traces from specific models, so I'm limited in what I can add without stepping outside the dataset.

Looking at the available models in HarmThoughts—DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Llama-8B, DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-32B, QwQ-32B, and OpenThinker-7B—including all of them is better than arbitrarily excluding one, even if OpenThinker is Qwen-based. I'm going with recommendation (a).

For C6, I should verify those citations are actually real. On S2, the key issue is pinning down whether the text baseline uses TF-IDF or sentence embeddings—I know from exp-013 it's sentence-level features, but I need to confirm which. S5 looks solid since BOCPD results from exp-014 provide negative evidence for Claim 3. S7 requires checking what's actually implemented in exp-003 for cross-model transfer. On S11, option (b) seems more pragmatic.

But there's a critical gap here: the layer selection metric issue—that `score = crossing_rate × lead_mean` formula is fundamentally flawed—isn't addressed anywhere in this matrix, and Claim 4 rests entirely on it. The Director needs to either add an action item to re-validate layer selection with an accuracy-aware metric or acknowledge Claim 4 might be invalid. There's also the R1-32B GRU accuracy problem at 59.5%—the matrix mentions GRU instability in terms of worst-seed reporting, but the real issue is that a near-random model's lead time numbers are meaningless. And R1-32B is still missing from the analysis.

On the specific questions: position-only baseline accuracy depends on checkpoint distribution, but if CPs cluster around 80% of the trace, I'd expect roughly 70-75% step-level accuracy just from predicting "safe" for the first three-quarters. For the K=5 crossing metric, position-only could hit moderate coverage but without precision gains over text, so selectivity needs to exceed 15 percentage points to be convincing. OpenThinker-7B is worth running, though I need to think through the implications. it worsens the Qwen-heavy issue (4/5 models are Qwen2.5-based). Still better than exclusion without justification. The value is completing the full HarmThoughts model set.

**Q3: CKA?**
Agree with (b). Scope down. CKA across different hidden dims is noisy and the claim doesn't need it.

**Q4: Score estimate after fixes?**
With C1-C6 + S1-S12 fixed, assuming C2 control task passes (selectivity > 15pp): I'd estimate 3.0-3.5/5. The paper becomes a solid empirical study. Main track depends on Claim 4 surviving (if it's a metric artifact, it doesn't) and Claim 1 being validated on all models. Findings track is very plausible.

**Q5: Missing items?**
A few things I need to address: the layer selection metric issue from my earlier critique, whether R1-32B's hidden state versus text bootstrap approach holds up, and the R1-32B GRU's 59.5% accuracy and whether that's actually meaningful. Let me work through these.

This is a CRITICAL finding!

The R1-32B HS vs text FPR bootstrap has already been run, and the results show:
- **HS pre-CP FPR = 25.7%**
- **Text pre-CP FPR = 24.6%**
- **Difference = +1.0% (HS is WORSE!)**
- **p = 0.848 (completely non-significant)**

This means **Claim 1 (precision advantage) DOES NOT hold on R1-32B!**

On QwQ-32B: HS 16.4% vs Text 31.2% (p=0.001) — strong advantage


On R1-32B: HS 25.7% vs Text 24.6% (p=0.848) — NO advantage at all

The precision advantage completely collapses on R1-32B, which undermines the paper's claim about universal cross-architecture benefits. The result was already computed on the server but isn't reflected in the registry, suggesting a disconnect between what's been run and what's being tracked.

Let me check the timestamp on this file to understand whether this was a recent experiment that hasn't been processed yet or something else.

But the result is devastating: **HS has NO precision advantage on R1-32B (p=0.848, difference +1.0%).**


This completely undermines the core claims — the 16% vs 27-36% FPR advantage only holds for two of the three models, not R1-32B where HS actually performs slightly worse. But I think I've found the connection: R1-32B uses layer 2, which was selected by that flawed metric I identified earlier. If layer 2 is genuinely noisy with only 62% accuracy, then its high FPR makes sense — the text baseline might actually be superior here because the layer selection process itself was broken. This ties everything together and shows how the methodological flaw directly breaks the paper's central claim. This needs to go out immediately.

<details open><summary>🔧 Tool call — mcp__agent-ml-research__reviewer_post_to_director · `toolu_011xJsBW4HPrAw5crq3idf7U`</summary>

```json
{
  "text": "[severity: warning] Action Matrix 独立审查 — 逐条评估 + 回答 5 个问题\n\n## 整体评估\n\nAction Matrix 质量高，逻辑清晰。但有一个关键遗漏：**layer selection 指标缺陷**（我之前两条 critical 已报告）未被纳入。结合刚发现的 R1-32B FPR 失败，这个遗漏升级为 blocking issue。\n\n---\n\n## 逐条审查\n\n### Critical Issues\n\n**C1 (Overclaim)**: ✅ 方案正确且充分。标题候选合理。\n\n**C2 (Control Task)**: ✅ 技术方案正确。补充评估：\n- Hewitt & Liang random-label control 是标准做法\n- Position-only baseline 设计正确\n- Director 的安全垫论证（HS vs text 使用相同 position，差异不可被 position 解释）逻辑成立——但前提是 Claim 1 本身成立（见 R1-32B 问题）\n- 资源估算合理（CPU only, ~1-2h）\n\n**C3 (Meta-analysis)**: ✅ 完全正确，k=3 不够做 meta-analysis。\n\n**C4 (数据划分)**: ✅ 我已确认代码实现：`split_traces()` 使用 60/20/20 (train/val/test)。但注意 r1-8b pipeline (`run_pipeline.py`) 调用 `split_data()`（来自 data_loader.py），而 32B 实验各自有独立的 split 函数。需确认两者一致。\n\n**C5 (OpenThinker-7B)**: 方案 (a) 技术可行，但有一个问题：OpenThinker-7B 是 Qwen2.5-7B-based → 跑完后模型分布变为 4/5 Qwen-based（r1-8b 是唯一 Llama）。这**加剧而非缓解** reviewer concern C1 \"Qwen-heavy\"。建议：\n- 仍然跑（数据越多越好）\n- 但 Limitations 需明确承认 4/5 Qwen-based，且解释原因（HarmThoughts 数据集可用模型决定）\n\n**C6 (Related Work)**: ✅ 引用列表正确且必要。注意 Hewitt & Liang 2019 同时服务 C2 和 C6。\n\n### Should-Fix Issues\n\n**S2 (Text baseline)**: 我已确认代码使用 `all-MiniLM-L6-v2` sentence embeddings（非 TF-IDF）。Paper 明确标注即可，无需重跑。\n\n**S5 (BOCPD 结果)**: exp-014 结果已在 registry（negative 结论），直接纳入论文。\n\n**S7 (Transfer projection head)**: 注意 `exp_001a_bootstrap_hs_vs_text.py` 代码显示 R1-32B 用的是独立训练的 LR probe（train on R1-32B data），不是 transfer。cross-transfer 实验是 exp-003（尚未完成）。区分清楚。\n\n**S11 (CKA)**: 同意 (b) scope down。跨维度 CKA (4096 vs 5120) 需投影，且 n 过小结果难 interpret。\n\n---\n\n## 回答 5 个问题\n\n**Q1: Position-only baseline 预期 accuracy / selectivity 安全线？**\n\n预期：如果 CP 集中在 ~75-85% trace 位置，position-only LR 可能达 65-70% step-level accuracy（因为\"predict safe for first 75% steps\"就有 75% baseline accuracy on class-balanced set，但实际是 imbalanced 的）。\n\n安全线：selectivity (real_acc - control_acc) > 10pp 是 minimum acceptable（Hewitt & Liang 原文建议 > 0 即可，但 reviewer 会更严格）。建议额外报告 **precision selectivity**（real_precision - position_precision），因为 precision 差异才是 Claim 1 的核心。\n\n**关键：即使 position-only accuracy 较高，HS vs text 的精度差异不可能被 position 解释**（两者 position 相同）。但如果 R1-32B 上精度差异本身不存在（p=0.848），position confound 就变成 Claim 1 唯一幸存模型（r1-8b + QwQ-32B）的问题了。\n\n**Q2: OpenThinker-7B 值得跑吗？**\n\n值得，但认清它的作用：\n- 补完 HarmThoughts 全集 → 排除\"选择性排除\"攻击\n- 提供 7B vs 32B within Qwen family 的 scale 对比\n- 不解决 architecture diversity 问题（仍 Qwen-heavy）\n- 如果时间紧张，优先级低于 layer selection 修正（后者可能挽救整个 Claim 1）\n\n**Q3: CKA 是否 must-have？**\n\n不是。Scope down claim + 承认 shared geometry 是 hypothesis 即可。p=0.848 的 R1-32B FPR 结果本身就否定了 \"shared geometry\" 的 naive interpretation。\n\n**Q4: 修完后能到几分？**\n\n取决于 layer selection 修正后 R1-32B 是否翻盘：\n- **乐观场景**（正确 layer 上 R1-32B FPR 显著）：3.5-4.0/5，Main track 有竞争力\n- **悲观场景**（R1-32B 仍不显著）：3.0-3.5/5，需降级为 \"2/3 models show advantage\"，Findings track 更现实\n- 当前不修 layer selection：2.5-3.0/5，基本持平审稿分\n\nC2 control task 是 pass/fail 门槛。通过 → 上述评分成立。失败（selectivity ≈ 0）→ 论文需要 fundamental restructure。\n\n**Q5: 遗漏问题？**\n\n1. **[BLOCKING] Layer selection 指标修正** — 我之前 2 条 critical 的核心。Action Matrix 完全未覆盖。R1-32B FPR 失败（p=0.848）很可能是因为使用了错误的层（layer 2, acc=62%）。修正 layer selection 是可能挽救 Claim 1 的唯一路径。建议提升为 C7 / P0 最高优先级，与 C2 并行。\n\n2. **Registry 数据错误** — exp_001a metrics 中 `best_layer_crossing: 0.474, best_layer_lead: 16.3` 是从 exp_001b 复制的错误数据。需修正。\n\n3. **R1-32B HS vs text 结果未入 registry** — 文件已存在但 registry 和 ROADMAP 均未反映。\n\n4. **r1-8b HS vs text 还缺独立 paired bootstrap** — 当前数据来自 exp-015 FPR analysis（非 bootstrap），ROADMAP 表中标 \"pending\"。需补跑才能完成 3-model 统计汇总。",
  "project": "contrastive_trace_safety"
}

```text
The registry has been updated for exp_001a_r1_32b. It now shows:
- `hs_precp_fpr: 0.2565`
- `text_precp_fpr: 0.2461`
- `bootstrap_hs_vs_text_fpr_p: 0.848`
- `bootstrap_hs_vs_text_fpr_diff: 0.0104`
- `bootstrap_hs_vs_text_fpr_ci95: [-0.0948, 0.1156]`


But the conclusion is marked "success" when it should be "negative" or "partial" — the p-value of 0.848 shows no significant precision advantage, the best layer depth is 3% not 44%, and this contradicts the original hypothesis about cross-architecture generalization. I'm also noticing that the old metrics like traces_extracted and best_layer have been completely replaced with just the bootstrap FPR data.

The registry entry was overwritten rather than updated, which means previous experimental results got deleted from that record. My main concern is that despite clear hypothesis falsification, the conclusion still reads as success. The critical R1-32B FPR finding is now confirmed in the registry, but I'm uncertain what happens next — whether the Agent will attempt the layer selection fix I suggested, accept this as a negative result, or take a different approach.

Since the bootstrap worker just completed and the registry already reflects my findings, I'll post a brief note acknowledging the update while flagging that the success label doesn't match the actual results.
```

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
I see exp_003_cross_transfer is the newest experiment in the registry, but I don't see the r1_8b_balanced_recompute. It may not have been registered yet, or it might be stored under a different name. Let me check on the server for the output.

Also, I notice exp_003_cross_transfer was just completed — this is the cross-model transfer experiment that's now showing as "negative" conclusion. Let me look at it:

Cross-transfer results:
- Baselines (within-model):
  - r1_8b: acc=0.8234, crossing=41.3%, lead=-1.46
  - r1_32b: acc=0.6516, crossing=63.3%, lead=+11.74
  - qwq_32b: acc=0.7171, crossing=72.4%, lead=+9.24
- Best transfers:
  - r1_8b → r1_32b: acc=0.522 (pca_2048) — near random
  - r1_8b → qwq_32b: acc=0.5948 (pca_2048) — weak
  - r1_32b → qwq_32b: acc=0.5499 (incomplete)

The transfer performance is significantly degraded compared to the baselines, which explains the negative conclusion on this experiment.

Looking at the qwq_32b → r1_32b transfer though, I'm seeing 0.6726 with direct transfer—no projection needed since both models are 5120-dimensional Qwen2.5-based architectures. That's the strongest result here, which makes sense given the architectural similarity, though even this best-case scenario still shows meaningful performance loss.

Wait, actually the qwq_32b probe transfers to r1_32b at 0.6726, which is higher than r1_32b's own baseline of 0.6516. That's counterintuitive—the cross-model probe is outperforming the within-model one. This could mean QwQ-32B had better training data or the baseline used a different setup. Let me check what the r1_8b_balanced_recompute worker found.

| Text temporal W=15 | 65.1% | +11.5 | 27.2% | 48.7% | 67.8% |


| HS static LR L14 | 60.5% | +3.0 | 16.2% | 62.2% | 76.8% |
| HS temporal LR W=15 | 65.1% | +4.9 | 16.0% | 58.3% | 72.6% |

| Method | Coverage | Lead | Pre-CP FPR | Precision | Acc |
|--------|----------|------|------------|-----------|-----|
| Text static | 34.9% | +9.8

Comparing the balanced results against the previous unbalanced configuration to see how class weighting affects the model performance across different methods. Looking at the performance metrics across these models, the balanced class weights approach is delivering significant improvements—the false positive rate for HS models dropped substantially from the 16% range down to around 9-10%, which is exactly what we need for Claim 1. The text-based models also improved their FPR, though the gap between HS and text approaches has remained relatively consistent at about 11 percentage points.

What's interesting is how the coverage and lead time metrics are shifting with temporal windows—HS models are showing better coverage with shorter windows (W=5 gives us 67.4% coverage), but the lead time advantage has nearly disappeared for the HS temporal approach at W=15, dropping from +4.9 to essentially zero. Meanwhile, text models are maintaining their lead time advantage around +10-11 days, and the GRU baseline remains stable. There's a critical trade-off emerging here that I need to examine more closely. R probes now have ~0 lead time!**
   - This means HS LR detects AT the commitment point, not BEFORE it
   - Only the GRU achieves positive lead (+4.5)
   - Text baseline still has +10.3 lead with lower precision

7. **The precision advantage is STRONGER with balanced**: HS precision 68-70% vs text 47% (was 58% vs 49%)

8. **GRU instability reduced**: coverage std=4.0% (was much higher previously), lead std=3.0

Now let me think about what this means for the paper:

**Good news:**
- HS pre-CP FPR drops to ~10% vs text ~21% — stronger precision story
- HS accuracy 81-82% vs text 69-73% — clean advantage
- GRU coverage stable at ~73% with +4.5 

The real tension here is that while the precision gap widened significantly, the LR probes lost their early detection capability—they're now firing right at the commitment point rather than ahead of it. The text baseline maintains a 10-step lead despite lower precision, which actually challenges the "early detection" framing. My precision advantage claim is definitely stronger with the FPR gap now at 10 percentage points, and the temporal context necessity still holds since the window-based approach substantially outperforms static features. But I need to reframe this around precise detection rather than early detection, and account for how the class weighting changes will shift the numbers in the final paper. ual detection unreliable): **CONFIRMED** — LR lead is ~0, GRU gives +4.5 but with variance

**Discrepancy with exp-015**: The new balanced results differ significantly from exp-015 numbers that are currently in paper_framing.md. This means the paper evidence table needs updating.

Let me also note: exp-015 used `class_weight=None` (default) for some probes? Or was it already balanced? Let me check... From the previous context, exp-015's code used `class_weight="balanced"` for LogisticRegression (I saw this in bootstrap_ci.py). But the results were different. The discrepancy might be due to:
- Different train/test split (exp-015 used a different split vs this recompute uses 60/20/20)
- Different detection thresholds
- Different evaluation methodology

The recompute script uses a 60/20/20 split with seed=42, which aligns with the 32B experiments, so exp-015 likely relied on an older split configuration from run_pipeline.py. With only 43 test traces, this methodological variation naturally produces different outcomes—something worth flagging in the paper.

I should focus my review on three points: confirming the numbers are solid and well-organized, highlighting the strengthened precision advantage, noting that the lead time issue is now clearer (LR essentially flat, GRU carrying the positive lead), and explaining the exp-015 discrepancy as a split difference rather than a computational error.

_source line 1040 · 2026-05-16T22:37:38.352Z_

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
I already saw exp_003_cross_transfer results in the registry when I checked earlier. Let me review the findings:

From the registry:
- Conclusion: "negative" (correct)
- Hypothesis: "Safety commitment probes trained on one reasoning model can transfer to other architectures/scales via simple dimension projection, because the safety signal is a universal representation rather than model-specific."

Results:
- Baselines (within-model):
  - r1_8b: acc=0.8234, crossing=41.3%, lead=-1.46
  - r1_32b: acc=0.6516, crossing=63.3%, lead=+11.74
  - qwq_32b: acc=0.7171, crossing=72.4%, lead=+9.24

The transfer attempts all performed poorly—most near random or barely above it. The only somewhat promising result was qwq_32b transferring back to r1_32b with moderate accuracy, but overall the hypothesis doesn't hold up. The safety signal doesn't appear to be a universal representation that transfers cleanly across these different models. - r1_32b → r1_8b: acc=0.5828 (pca_1024) — weak

Key observations:
1. Cross-architecture transfer (Llama → Qwen or 4096-d → 5120-d) is near random (52-59%)
2. Same-architecture same-dimension transfer (QwQ-32B → R1-32B, both Qwen2.5 5120-d) works moderately (67.3% vs 65.2% baseline) — actually beats the self-probe slightly
3. The "negative" conclusion is appropriate

One interesting finding: qwq_32b → r1_32b (0.6726) outperforms r1_32b's own baseline (0.6516). Both are 5120-d Qwen2.5 models with direct transfer, suggesting QwQ-32B's representations might be better suited for this task than the self-baseline.

I'm noticing the baselines here differ from the balanced recompute — r1_8b shows acc=0.8234 with 41.3% crossing, but the balanced version had 82.0% accuracy and 67.4% coverage. That crossing rate gap is substantial, likely indicating different layer or window configurations between experiments. I should verify the actual configuration used in this experiment, though the registry already confirms the "negative" conclusion is sound. The real question is whether this finding supports or contradicts any claims in the paper. - Claim 4 (scale-dependent signal) is partially supported: if representations were universal, transfer would work. Transfer fails → representations are model-specific. But this is a WEAK form of evidence for Claim 4.

Let me check if there's anything else to investigate, or if my prior review of this data is sufficient.

Actually, I notice something: the baselines in this exp_003 seem different from the balanced recompute results:
- r1_8b baseline here: acc=0.8234, crossing=0.4127, lead=-1.46
- r1_8b balanced recompute HS Temporal W=5: acc=0.820, coverage=67.4%, lead=+0.6

The coverage difference is striking: 41.3% vs 67.4%. This suggests the baseline in exp_003 is using a different configuration—maybe a static probe or different window/layer settings. I need to dig into the detailed results to understand what's driving this discrepancy.
```

_source line 1045 · 2026-05-16T22:38:04.696Z_

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
Now I have the full cross-transfer results. Let me analyze this carefully:

**Key observations:**

1. **Baselines use ALL data (no 60/20/20 split per model):**
   - r1_8b: n_traces=63 (test only from 209 total? Or full dataset minus val?)
   - r1_32b: n_traces=30 (test from 100)
   - qwq_32b: n_traces=29 (test from 95)

2. **Transfer accuracy matrix:**


Looking at the cross-transfer performance, I'm seeing some interesting patterns. The diagonal shows baseline performance, while off-diagonal entries reveal how well models trained on one system generalize to another. The qwq_32b → r1_32b transfer is particularly notable at 0.6726, which appears to use a direct evaluation approach.

However, there's a critical issue here: this high accuracy is deceptive. The probe is essentially predicting "safe" almost everywhere, which inflates accuracy on imbalanced data where most steps are indeed safe. The crossing rate is only 1%, meaning it almost never detects actual commitment points—it's essentially a useless classifier despite the impressive-looking accuracy number.

In contrast, the r1_32b → qwq_32b transfer shows the opposite problem: 0.3848 accuracy (worse than random for binary classification) with a crossing rate of 1.0, meaning it fires on every single trace. This is equally problematic but in the opposite direction—it's overly sensitive and unreliable.

The PCA-based transfers fall somewhere in between, ranging from near-random to slightly above-chance performance with low detection rates. Looking at all these results together, the "negative" conclusion is clearly correct: transfer learning isn't working meaningfully here, which means the hypothesis about shared geometric structure across models doesn't hold up.

The real problem is how the registry presents these findings—it highlights the 67.3% accuracy as the "best transfer" result without flagging that this comes from a degenerate classifier that essentially never detects anything.  The genuinely useful transfers perform worse than random, which directly contradicts the shared geometry idea. For the paper, this is actually valuable: it's a clean negative result that supports the claim that representations are specific to their model and scale, not universal. The transfer matrix should be reported honestly as evidence against the shared geometry hypothesis rather than buried or misframed.

I'm noticing the r1_8b baseline has much higher crossing rate (41%) compared to the temporal recompute results (67%), which suggests they're using different configurations entirely — possibly a static probe versus temporal, or probing a different layer. I need to check what setup the exp_003 baselines actually used to understand this discrepancy.
```

_source line 1049 · 2026-05-16T22:38:21.426Z_

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
Now I see the configuration:
- r1_8b: layer_idx=2 (which is layer 14 in the model, stored as index 2 in the [12..24] subset)
- r1_32b: layer_idx=2 (the "best" layer from the flawed metric)
- qwq_32b: layer_idx=1 (the "best" layer from the flawed metric)

This is a **static probe** (no temporal windowing) — just raw hidden states at a single step mapped to labels. That explains why the baselines look different from the temporal probe results.


The transfer experiment is using those same problematic layer selections for the 32B models, so if they're actually probing noisy early layers, the transfer failure might just reflect that noise doesn't generalize across models rather than proving representations are fundamentally model-specific.

But the 8B-to-32B transfer from a strong layer 14 still fails at 52.2%, which is a cleaner test of cross-architecture transfer and shows it breaks down regardless of source quality. Meanwhile, the qwq_32b → r1_32b "direct" transfer reports 67.3% accuracy but has a crossing rate of 0.01—the registry flags this as "best" without noting the probe almost never activates, making it useless for actual detection despite the decent accuracy numbers.

The "negative" conclusion is warranted, but the core issues are that the registry's "best_transfer" metric conflates accuracy with crossing rate, and degenerate classifiers that fire constantly or almost never should be filtered out.
```

</details>

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
Excellent! The layer selection fix has been applied. Let me analyze the results:

**R1-32B (exp_001a):**
- Old best layer: 2 (3% depth, acc=62%)
- **Corrected best layer: 10 (15.6% depth)**
- Layer 10: bal_acc=0.718, crossing_rate=0.6
- Metric used: `balanced_accuracy × crossing_rate` (scheme B) — exactly what I recommended

GRU results on corrected layers:
- Single: bal_acc=0.675, crossing=30%, lead=+9.1, pre-CP FPR=13.5%


- Multi: bal_acc=0.655, crossing=23%, lead=+8.8, pre-CP FPR=11.4%

Temporal LR performs best at W=10 with bal_acc=0.737 and 50% crossing rate, though the pre-CP FPR climbs to 18.9%. Now comparing HS versus text bootstrap on the corrected layer—HS shows a pre-CP FPR of 26.0% versus text's 24.6%, so HS is still underperforming by 1.4 percentage points with very high confidence.

For QwQ-32B, the corrected best layer shifted only slightly from layer 1 to layer 2, and the GRU models show strong crossing rates above 75% with pre-CP FPR values around 28-30%, while Temporal LR continues to be evaluated. Looking at the QwQ-32B metrics more carefully, I see the temporal LR shows 18% pre-CP FPR but there's no fresh bootstrap comparison with text since the layer only shifted from 1 to 2 — the previous bootstrap is still approximately valid but should ideally be re-validated on the corrected layer. For R1-32B, the corrected layer 10 (15.6% depth) is more reasonable than layer 2, with better balanced accuracy and crossing rates, though it's still quite different from the R1-8B pattern. The striking finding is that even with the corrected layer, HS shows no significant precision advantage on R1-32B (p=0.999), which suggests the HS benefit may not generalize across model sizes. The layer depth pattern across models is less uniform than initially thought—ranging from 44% down to 3%—which undermines the "scale-dependent dynamics" claim since there's no consistent progression. When I recalculate the GRU results on R1-32B with the corrected layer, coverage drops sharply from 67% to 23-30% and the lead decreases from +11.2 to +8.8-9.1, though the pre-CP false positive rate improves substantially to around 13.5%.

For the temporal LR on R1-32B, I'm seeing a discrepancy: the bootstrap reports an HS false positive rate of 26% while the temporal LR at window 10 shows 18.9%, which suggests they might be using different layer depths or configurations that I need to reconcile.

But ultimately, the statistical test is clear — p=0.999 means HS isn't outperforming text on R1-32B across any of the configurations I've tested, so I should move forward with writing up my findings.
```

_source line 1073 · 2026-05-16T22:59:55.770Z_

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
Now I have the full picture of the layer selection fix results. Let me analyze:

**R1-32B (corrected, best layer=10):**
- Single-layer GRU:
  - Mean: bal_acc=67.5%, crossing=30%, lead=+9.1, pre-CP FPR=13.5%
  - Range: crossing 20-35%, FPR 9.4-16.3%
- Multi-layer GRU (layers 6-14):
  - Mean: bal_acc=65.5%, crossing=23.3%, lead=+8.8, pre-CP FPR=11.4%
  - Range: crossing 5-40%, FPR 4.5-18.9% — the multi-layer setup is extremely unstable, with crossing rates swinging wildly from 5% to 40% depending on the seed
  - Bootstrap testing shows no meaningful difference between HS and text representations (26% vs 24.6%, p=0.999)

**QwQ-32B (corrected, best layer=2):**
- Single-layer GRU performs much better here with bal_acc=69.4%, crossing=75.4%, lead=+19.3, and pre-CP FPR=30.3%, showing strong coverage
- Multi-layer GRU (layers 0-6) pushes crossing to 84.2% with bal_acc=72.4% and lead=+15.9, though FPR stays elevated at 28.9%, and importantly this is much more stable across seeds — all reaching 84% crossing
- The old HS vs text bootstrap comparison on layer 1 showed a significant gap (16.4% vs 31.2%, p=0.001), but I haven't run a new comparison on layer 2 yet

The layer selection fix didn't substantially change QwQ-32B's performance — switching from layer 1 to layer 2 had minimal impact since both are very early layers anyway, so the original metrics were already fairly representative.

For R1-32B, the fix moved the optimal layer from 2 to 10, which is much more reasonable depth-wise, but the HS vs text bootstrap comparison still shows no significant difference (p=0.999) — so the layer correction didn't resolve the Claim 1 issue. The dramatic coverage drop from 66.7% down to 23-30% reveals that the old high coverage was just noise from early-layer features firing indiscriminately.

QwQ-32B's GRU remains solid with 84% coverage and +16 lead time, though the ~29% pre-CP FPR indicates it's overly sensitive — catching most crossing events but generating substantial false alarms. The two 32B models are showing quite different behaviors now that I've corrected the layer selection. The layer fix validates my findings: r1-8b and QwQ-32B show the expected FPR advantage for HS over text, but R1-32B doesn't—it's a genuine negative result that the paper should report honestly, possibly reflecting something about how that model was distilled.
```

{
  "text": "[severity: warning] 审查报告 — m001 layer selection fix\n\n## 修正执行情况\n\nLayer selection 指标已改为 `balanced_accuracy × crossing_rate`（scheme B），符合我的建议。\n\n| 模型 | 旧 best layer | 修正后 best layer | 旧 metric | 新 metric |\n|------|-------------|-----------------|-----------|-----------|\n| R1-32B | 2 (3%) | **10 (15.6%)** | crossing×lead | bal_acc×crossing |\n| QwQ-32B | 1 (1.6%) | **2 (3.1%)** | crossing×lead | bal_acc×crossing |\n\nR1-32B 层选择明显改善（更深、更准确）。QwQ-32B 几乎不变。\n\n## R1-32B 修正后关键结果\n\n### [warning] HS vs text 精度优势仍不成立（悲观场景确认）\n\n即使在修正后的 layer 10 上：\n- HS pre-CP FPR = 26.0%\n- Text pre-CP FPR = 24.6%\n- **p = 0.999（完全不显著）**\n- CI: [-10.3%, +12.7%]\n\n**结论：R1-32B 的 HS 对 text 无精度优势是该模型的固有特性，非 layer selection 的 artifact。** Claim 1 必须限定为\"2/3 models (r1-8b + QwQ-32B)\"。\n\n### [info] GRU 特性变化\n\nR1-32B GRU 在 layer 10 上变为\"保守检测器\"：\n- 单层：coverage=30%, lead=+9.1, **pre-CP FPR=13.5%**\n- 多层（6-14）：coverage=23%, lead=+8.8, **pre-CP FPR=11.4%**\n\n低 coverage 但低 FPR。与 QwQ-32B GRU 形成对比（coverage=84%, FPR=29%）。\n\n### [warning] R1-32B GRU 极不稳定\n\nMulti-layer GRU crossing rate 跨 seed：5% / 25% / 40%。seed 42 仅检测到 1 条 trace（5% of 20）。这使 R1-32B GRU 的所有统计数据不可靠。\n\n## QwQ-32B 修正后关键结果\n\n### [info] Layer 几乎未变，结果大体保持\n\nMulti-layer GRU（layers 0-6）：bal_acc=72.4%, crossing=84%, lead=+15.9, FPR=28.9%。\n高 coverage + 高 lead，但 FPR 偏高。\n\n### [warning] 缺少修正后的 HS vs text bootstrap\n\n旧 bootstrap（layer 1, W=10: 16.4% vs 31.2%, p=0.001）。修正后 layer=2, temporal LR W=5 pre-CP FPR=18%。需要在 layer 2 上重跑 HS vs text paired bootstrap 确认 p-value 仍显著。考虑到层变化极小（1→2），大概率仍成立，但严格来说需要验证。\n\n## 对 paper framing 的建议\n\n1. **Claim 1 降级**：\"Hidden-state probes achieve more precise detection on 2 of 3 models tested (r1-8b: 10% vs 21%; QwQ-32B: 16% vs 31%, p=0.001). On DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-32B, no significant difference was observed (p=0.999), possibly reflecting properties of knowledge distillation.\"\n\n2. **Claim 4 需重写**：层深度模式为 44% / 15.6% / 3.1% — 无 monotonic scale relationship，更像 model-specific。\"Scale-dependent\" framing 不成立。改为 \"model-specific optimal probe depth, requiring per-model layer selection\"。\n\n3. **exp_001a conclusion 仍标 \"success\"** — 假设是 \"cross-architecture generalization\"，实际结果明确否定。应为 \"negative\" 或 \"partial\"。",

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
This is the critical C2 control task experiment. Let me analyze in detail:

## 1. Selectivity (Hewitt & Liang random-label control)

| Model | Real Acc | Random Acc (mean) | **Selectivity** | CI |
|-------|----------|-------------------|----------------|-----|
| r1-8b | 85.4% | 73.0% | **12.5%** | ±0.4% |
| R1-32B | 67.6% | 50.8% | **16.8%** | ±1.6% |


| QwQ-32B | 75.8% | 56.9% | **18.9%** | ±0.9% |

All three models show selectivity above 10 percentage points, which is encouraging—the probes are picking up genuine safety patterns rather than just memorizing random noise. That said, there's something odd here: R1-32B's random baseline sits at 50.8%, essentially random chance for a binary task, meaning those random labels provide no signal. Its real accuracy of 67.6% gives a selectivity of 16.8%, but the probe itself isn't performing particularly well. Meanwhile, r1-8b's random baseline is surprisingly high at 73%, which suggests something different is happening with how those labels are being processed.

I'm wondering if the class imbalance is playing a role—most steps are labeled "safe" while post-checkpoint steps are rare, so even random labels might let the model exploit that distribution. But that doesn't quite explain it either, since truly random 50/50 labels should yield 50% accuracy. The 73% baseline for r1-8b hints that either the "random" labels aren't actually random, or there's information leaking from the features themselves. Given that we're working with PCA-500 features, which might encode positional information, the probe could be learning patterns in the data structure even when labels are shuffled, especially if it picks up any correlation between position and the feature space.

The Hewitt & Liang control shuffles the label-to-input mapping, and with 73% accuracy on random labels, that's surprisingly high—it suggests the hidden state features contain substantial structure independent of the true labels. Still, the selectivity gap matters: r1-8b gets only 12.5 percentage points from random to real accuracy, while r1-32b achieves 16.8pp. Both are well above zero, which is what the control requires, so all three models pass this test.

Now I'm looking at the false positive rates when using only position information versus the hidden states. The key finding is that r1-8b's hidden state FPR of 8.1% is actually half the position-only baseline of 15.8%—hidden states are doing substantially better than position alone. R1-32B shows a different pattern where position-based detection is actually more effective. HS 31.8% ≈ Position 2D 31.3% — NO advantage over position!
- QwQ-32B: HS 17.2% < Position temporal 21.8% — moderate advantage

So for r1-8b: the precision advantage is NOT explained by position (HS FPR 8.1% vs position FPR 15.8%).
For R1-32B: HS doesn't beat position (HS 31.8% ≈ position 31.3%) — the probe might just be learning position!
For QwQ-32B: moderate advantage (HS 17.2% vs position 21.8%)

Now I'm looking at the checkpoint position distribution across models. The data shows r1-8b has a mean of 70.5% and median of 86.1%, with high variance across all three models. Most checkpoints cluster very late in the trace—r1-8b especially has 87 out of 209 traces in the 0.9-1.0 bucket. This right-skewed distribution explains why position-only baselines work reasonably well; they can just predict safe early and unsafe late and capture most of the pattern.

I'm noticing the selectivity experiment applies different layers across models, and I need to verify r1-8b is using the correct layer 14 per the balanced recomputation approach. Looking at the position baseline data more carefully, R1-32B shows HS achieving 31.8% FPR versus 37.0% for temporal position — a modest 5.2 percentage point advantage, much smaller than the 7.7pp gap I see in R1-8B. Despite the control task running on the wrong layer for R1-32B, all three models still pass the core validation that HS probes detect safety commitment beyond position alone, though the effect sizes vary considerably across the models.

The position temporal results also suggest why text embeddings struggle with high FPR — they may be partly encoding positional information since sentence content shifts predictably through a trace, making them more vulnerable to position confounding than hidden states. Notably, position temporal achieves 100% crossing rate on R1-8B with lead=+2.5, meaning it detects something on every trace but lacks precision, whereas the HS probe is far more selective at 67.4% crossing rate.

I should note that R1-32B was tested on an older layer 2 configuration, so I need to rerun it on layer 10 for a proper comparison, though the selectivity test likely still holds given layer 10's higher accuracy.
```
